[00:42:19] You need others to step in and open doors and remove barriers. So that's what we do. We remove barriers and we open doors and we inspire them with hope. One of my favorite quotes of a graduate was a young woman who said, Homestretch will not change your life.

[01:13:57] Thank you, Supervisor Gross. Next up is Supervisor Walkinshaw. thank you mr chairman i have one written board matter and a couple of announcements the written board matter is a joint board matter with uh jointly with chairman mckay vice chairman gross supervisor stork and supervisor palchick to send a letter to governor youngkin regarding the commonwealth's withdrawal from the regional greenhouse gas initiative mr chairman in 2020 the general assembly passed and the governor signed the virginia clean energy and community flood preparedness act it directed the virginia department of environmental quality to establish

[01:14:32] a carbon dioxide cap-and-trade program to reduce emissions. It also authorized Virginia to link that program to the Regional Greenhouse Gas Initiative , a cooperative effort among 12 eastern states from Maine to Virginia to reduce CO2 emissions from power plants.

[01:14:52] Together, the states established a regional limit on emissions from regulated power plants within their territories. It allows regulated facilities to purchase allowances for each ton of emissions, and it gives electric generating facilities the options to use their allowances to comply with annual limits, bank them for future use, or trade them to other electric generation facilities.

[01:15:16] During a three-year period, each state offers its allowances for sale in quarterly auctions based on established budgets to meet their targets. Over time, the regional cap or budget declines so that those emissions are decreased in a planned and predictable way. Since its inception, emissions from plants in those states have declined by more than 50%, twice as fast as the nation as a whole, and the program has raised nearly $6 billion that's been invested in local communities. For its part, Virginia has received more than $650 million in just the last two years. Virginia has two major requirements for

[01:15:55] its participation. One, 50% of the money that comes to Virginia is required to be spent on energy efficiency programs in low-income communities, including programs right here in Fairfax County. And 45% is to go toward helping communities that are adversely affected by flooding or sea level rise, including communities right here in Fairfax County. Here in Fairfax County, we've received more than $15.4 million worth of benefits from the state's participation in the program. A small portion of that has been a valuable source of revenue for our community Housing Partners program that works with low-income property owners to provide energy-saving home

[01:16:34] improvements to reduce costs. A loss of RGGI funding to this program would impact not just Fairfax County residents, but 131,000 low-income residents across the state. Researchers have estimated that the loss of funding would mean those homeowners and renters would not be able to save. They would miss out on saving an average of $540 per year on their utility bills.

[01:17:00] Furthermore, the biggest share of RGGI funding coming to Fairfax County went to our Department of Public Works and Environmental Services to supplement the $7 million it was budgeted last year. So we budgeted $7 million, and that money has been supplemented with RGGI funds to address flood mitigation needs in the county across most of our districts.

[01:17:23] It's been a significant source of assistance to meeting our flood mitigation and safety goals and also supporting our resilient Fairfax goals. Leaving RGGI, as the governor is attempting to do, would jeopardize this critical funding to protect Fairfax County residents from flooding.

[01:17:41] Beyond the financial benefits, our participation also supports our local emissions reduction goals that are described in our CCAP. And as many of you know, as I noted, the governor took steps and is taking steps to remove Virginia from the RGGI program. He's described it as a, quote, regressive tax, which does not do anything to incentivize the reduction of emissions in Virginia.

[01:18:04] As I noted earlier, the numbers show the opposite. RGGI is working to reduce emissions and improve air quality in our region, and millions of dollars are going to help residents to reduce their energy costs and minimize the impacts of climate change.

[01:18:23] As such, Mr. Chairman, we think it's necessary to send a letter to the governor requesting that he end his efforts to withdraw Virginia from RGGI. A program that aligns with this board's goals has helped reduce flood risk, lowers energy bills for low-income residents, and increases the quality of the air that we breathe.

[01:18:43] Therefore, I move that the Board of Supervisors authorize the county executive to direct legislative staff to draft a letter to the governor, signed by the chairman, providing comments on the proposed withdrawal of the Commonwealth of Virginia from the Regional Greenhouse Gas Initiative. So I'm happy to second that, along with Supervisor Stork, Supervisor Gross, Supervisor Palchik, Supervisor Lusk, Supervisor Alcorn.

[01:19:07] Discussion on the motion, Supervisor Walkinshaw. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I think I covered most of it there. Obviously, this is really important. The governor has moved pretty far down this road, and there is a lawsuit pending and a very legitimate legal question as to whether he has the authority to do this without the General Assembly acting.

[01:19:32] But I think it makes sense to send him a letter, and maybe he can avoid all that lawsuit and just say, you know what, it turns out this isn't something that makes sense. I'll say even I was a little bit surprised. I didn't know how much money was coming directly to Fairfax County for us to help implement our programs in terms of flood mitigation. Our next public hearing is a public hearing on the proposed affordable housing preservation administrative policy guidelines.

[04:41:43] And as soon as staff is ready, we would recognize staff for a staff presentation.

[04:42:11] Thank you, Mr. Chairman. My name is Brianne Fuller with Housing and Community Development. I'm joined here today by Megan Van Dam and Tom Fleetwood from Housing and Community Development as well. And we have Tracy Strunk here from Department of Planning and Development as well. I'm here today to present to you the Administrative Policy Guidelines for Affordable Housing Preservation.

[04:42:32] For a little bit of background, the Affordable Housing Preservation Policy adopted in March sets forth a goal of one-for-one replacement of affordable units that are being redeveloped. And it offers additional density to assist in offsetting the impacts that that preservation has on the redevelopment proposal.

[04:42:52] Since the adoption of the policy in March, we have created administrative guidelines that include details of the process that are not laid out in the comprehensive plan itself. The administrative guidelines outline how property owners will work with the county to utilize the policy and provide direction on the administration of the preserved units after an entitlement is approved.

[04:43:17] We conducted engagement during the spring and summer to solicit feedback on the proposed guidelines. We hosted two virtual engagement sessions available to the public. We sent property owners letters to create awareness around the guidelines as well as those engagement sessions, posted targeted meetings with members of industry, and solicited feedback from the Board of Supervisors at the Housing Committee meetings in May and August.

[04:43:43] A draft of the guidelines was available on our website for the entire month of June, along with a public comment form so that people could give us direct feedback. We also included the links to the engagement sessions, and a recording of one of the sessions is available on the website as well.

[04:44:00] We did hear positive feedback regarding the determination of applicability and the factors that we will be using to determine if a site has affordable units on it. There were also positive comments about the ability to opt into the policy for market rate properties that don't fall under the definition of market affordable in the comprehensive plan, but do have rents that are up to 80% of the area median income.

[04:44:25] The major theme of feedback that we received for outreach for both the plan amendment and the guidelines was the need for predictability in the process. We are looking to put our current turnaround times for affordability assessments and preservation proposals on our website to assist that predictability as those timeframes were specifically requested during engagement.

[04:44:48] And this will help provide some predictability while also taking staff capacity into consideration so that we can provide a realistic look at what that turnaround might look like. We also received positive feedback about having enough flexibility in the guidelines to provide for a variety of different scenarios that might occur.

[04:45:07] The administrative guidelines and the policy it supports were designed to find harmony between both predictability and flexibility, which can sometimes compete with one another. We also discussed with the public the provision of relocation guidelines based on the Fairfax County relocation guidelines.

[04:45:25] Sorry, relocation plans based on the relocation guidelines. After receiving feedback, we made some additions to the document, as well as clarified various items, including the rents used in determining applicability of the policy, as well as the provision of units that are going to be off-site.

[04:45:45] The administrative guidelines include the applicability of the policy to rezoning cases, the review process for preservation proposal when the policy is to be used, the goals for the replacement units, the administration of preserved units after the entitlement process, and the development of relocation plans to assist existing tenants during the redevelopment process.

[04:46:12] The first topic in the administrative guidelines is the determination of policy applicability. As early on as possible in the process, HCD would determine if the policy applies to a project. Committed units that would qualify for the policy are affordable to households earning 80% of the AMI and below, either currently or whose rent restrictions have expired within the past 12 months.

[04:46:38] Those commitments might be through the ADU or WDU programs, they may have utilized tax credits, or have those rent restrictions through other means. Qualified market affordable units are those that are affordable to households earning 60% of the AMI and below and do not have rent restrictions.

[04:46:56] We will look at rents over the course of the preceding three years to determine if the property had market affordable units for a majority of that time frame. Another factor is if the property has appeared on one or more of the past two market affordable surveys that HCD conducts every two years.

[04:47:16] Properties that have market rents up to 80% may also opt into the policy, which can give them the benefit of additional density and can provide the county with additional affordable units that would not otherwise have been captured in the policy. This gives an option to preserve more workforce housing at higher affordability levels that are still very critical to providing housing for all income levels.

[04:47:40] HCD staff will utilize the average rents of multifamily buildings in the county that are gathered on a monthly basis, and we may also request rent information from the applicant as needed. The low-income housing tax credit rent thresholds will be used in determining applicability, and in terms of utilities, particularly with those market rents, utilities may or may not be included, and we will seek to understand what those rents do include as we are assessing a property.

[04:48:07] The information regarding applicability will be delivered via an affordability assessment. Affordability assessments should be requested by the property owner as early on in the process as possible.

[04:48:23] They will include the determination of whether or not the units meet the definitions of market affordable or committed affordable, as well as the AMI levels, number of units, and mix of bedrooms, which will then help inform the replacement unit goals.

[04:48:38] The next topic in the guidelines is the review of preservation proposals prior to a rezoning application being submitted or an authorization of a comprehensive plan amendment. After the affordability assessment is completed, the applicant will submit a preservation proposal for HCD and DPD to review.

[04:48:59] This will include the density needed to achieve one-for-one replacement based on the affordability assessment. It should also include any alternatives that are being proposed if they are necessary. HCD and DPD may request additional information, including massing concepts to give us an idea of what that additional density might look like on the site, and financial feasibility that shows the justification for any alternatives to one-for-one replacement.

[04:49:27] Preservation proposals should also include any information on potential off-site units. This would include a justification for locating units off-site and specific information regarding location of those units. The guidelines include parameters for off-site location, including that units should be within a one-mile radius of the original site and should have comparable access to major roads, transit, development centers, schools, and other amenities.

[04:49:58] The next topic in the guidelines covers the goals for the replacement units. The goal of the policy is to maintain affordability and minimize displacement of existing residents. So for replacement units, we are ultimately looking to seek the same number of units that exist on site with comparable AMI levels and number of bedrooms.

[04:50:19] We do also want to be able to meet the needs of the existing residents who would want to return to the redeveloped property. There might be tenants on that site that are underhoused, meaning that there isn't enough space for the occupants that live in a unit currently. They may be underhoused due to not being able to afford that unit individually or as a family.

[04:50:40] This is a concern we came across during public outreach for the plan amendment. And thus, the needs of the existing residents should be evaluated while creating the goals for the replacement units as well. The fourth topic in the administrative guidelines is the administration of the preserved units after the zoning process is complete.

[04:51:02] If the units are not already subject to another administration process for a state, local, or federal program, which can include utilizing tax credits or RHA financing, then the units would be administered using the workforce dwelling unit administrative guidelines.

[04:51:19] This would apply to processes including offering agreements and covenants, compliance, integration, dispersion of units, and unit sizing. The benefit of this mechanism is that we then have units committed through redevelopment being processed in a similar manner, and if something changes in the WDU guidelines, it would apply to this policy as well.

[04:51:42] The final topic in the guidelines is the provision of relocation assistance plans. Relocation assistance plans will be provided to HCD for review and approval during the entitlement process and should be crafted using the Fairfax County Relocation Guidelines if the property is not already subject to another program that has its own relocation requirements.

[04:52:05] The guidelines provide a framework for effective relocation plans and recommend elements that should be included in the plans. The plan should aim to minimize displacement of existing tenants and encourage fair and equitable treatment of anyone who is to be displaced for any period of time.

[04:52:23] So our next steps include meeting with members of industry to discuss spreading awareness of the policy. During our outreach for the guidelines, there was a good discussion with the NVBIA and NAOP members regarding increasing awareness of the policy and helping property owners to understand the potential benefits that the policy might have.

[04:52:45] So we do plan to continue those discussions to determine how we can assist with that messaging. We will also send letters to property owners and make them aware of any actions taken today and potentially start any conversations regarding preservation and or redevelopment.

[04:53:03] HCD staff are also currently working on an update to the relocation guidelines and plan to present an update to the Housing Committee in November. This is where we will address issues, including the right of tenants to return to the property and provisions for any temporary or permanent relocation. We will be working on our next market affordable survey in 2024, which is completed every two years to provide us with a set of data regarding how many market affordable units there are in the county at a given time.

[04:53:31] We are also preparing for the next preservation-related plan amendment, which is for manufactured housing specifically. And additional ongoing efforts include monitoring market rents monthly and seeking out opportunities to preserve affordable units with our bench of preservation partners.

[04:53:49] The administrative guidelines will assist us with preserving units that would otherwise lose their affordability through redevelopment. And the administrative guidelines will help guide us through the use of our policy that was adopted in March and complements the policy itself with additional details that are really crucial for understanding this process.

[04:54:11] And together they make up one more necessary tool in our toolbox for affordable housing. And thus, we ask the Board of Supervisors to adopt the administrative policy guidelines for affordable housing preservation. That concludes my presentation, and I am happy to take any questions.

[04:54:27] Thank you very much for that presentation. Questions from board members, and I'm going to start with Supervisor Gross. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. [06:08:31] It is a public hearing on a proposed zoning ordinance amendment regarding Article 6, parking and loading and related changes. And as I mentioned there, right now, I have 33 speakers signed up to testify.

[06:08:50] And so we are going to try to be as efficient as we possibly can with this. And at this time, I am going to recognize our county staff to give us a staff presentation. Thank you. We're excited to talk about Park and Reimagined tonight.

[06:09:07] My name is William Malin, Department of Planning and Development. A cool lead to joining me today are Michael Davis with Land Development Services and Austin Gassero at DPD. In addition, we have Tracy Strunk, the Director of Department of Planning and Development, Leslie Johnson, the Zoning Administrator, Bill Hicks, the Director of Land Development Services, and for our Consultant Team, Ian Banks of Nelson-Igaard.

[06:09:27] Our presentation will focus on the proposed changes and reasons for the changes for parking and loading. It should be noted that parking and reimagined is intended for new development and redevelopment, and existing developments would generally not be impacted. Almost two years ago in October, we officially kicked off this project with a joint meeting with the Planning Christian and Board.

[06:09:49] Throughout this project, engagement with the community has been a critical goal. We've had more than 100 meetings taking place, including 19 public halls, town halls, or open houses, meetings with board committees, neighborhoods, community, industry groups, our project work group, various county agencies, municipalities, board and land use committees, and individual members.

[06:10:08] We've used those meetings to find and redefine our proposal. Over a year ago in June, we first released draft rates with footnotes explaining the proposed changes. In general, we wanted the rates to be easier to understand, enforceable, right-size these rates, and combine rates with similar uses.

[06:10:26] Ten months ago, last November, we released a draft of the text, the detailed white paper explaining the proposed changes. Based on comments from the Community Staff Planning and Education Board, we made edits to the text in February, March, and in June. With each release, we drafted a paper explaining these proposed changes.

[06:10:44] This screen defines the challenges of our current parking regulations and responds to staff or proposals with the project. Many of these responses are provided to address comments we have heard during community outreach. The responses include developing parking regulations using a tiered framework that aligns with the context of land use in Fairfax County and uses a comprehensive plan as a basis for these tiers.

[06:11:08] Our proposal reflects an examination of parking requirements for all land uses and includes a reasonable right-sizing and simplification of most of these rates, many of which have not been updated since 1988 or even 1978. This is accomplished by combining rates with similar impacts to permit users to easily occupy the same space.

[06:11:27] For example, we propose to permit an office rate similar to our shopping center rate, so tenants can easily move in and out of an office suite and not require parking tabulation. Another example is permitting buy-right commercial revitalization rate, which encourages redevelopment of these areas.

[06:11:44] We've sought ways to be more flexible in designing and providing parking while addressing environmental and safety concerns. This includes pedestrian routes and permitting parking to be reduced to encourage additional landscaping when retrofitting an existing parking lot. When this project was initiated, we identified some core community values that we intended to address.

[06:12:04] The proposed changes to Article 6 create broad opportunities to positively support the goals of One Fairfax, Resilient Fairfax, and CCAP. One Fairfax promotes the equitable goal of creating a quality built environment, which accommodates anticipated growth and promotes housing and community services for all people.

[06:12:21] Reducing parking requirements allows unneeded parking space to be repurposed to help create these environments. Creating opportunities for more housing, which lowers its overall price, as well as more affordable housing options, is a critical goal within the county. Over-acquiring parking plays a role in limiting these options.

[06:12:38] The cost to provide parking has a direct effect on the ability to provide affordable housing. Residents pay for parking they do not need and opportunities are lost to use that unneeded parking for better purposes. Right-sizing of parking requirements enhances placemaking opportunities and helps to create better design sites where people want to be, such as the Kaplan site in Tyson's, Reston Town Center, and the Mosaic District. By right-sizing parking requirements and implementing proposals such as pedestrian routes and required bicycle parking, we can promote safer sites where people feel comfortable using improved pedestrian infrastructure.

[06:13:15] This project supports county goals environmental stewardship through both direct and indirect environmental benefit. There are no net environmental benefits gained from over-requiring parking. Lowering of parking requirements resulting in less required and pervious surface county-wide is an environmental benefit.

[06:13:33] Implementation of bicycle parking requirements and the creation of a tiered parking framework supports CCAB strategies by promoting the use of existing and proposed public transportation. Lowering parking requirements decreases required parking infrastructure in the form of impervious surfaces across all portions of the county, while also creating opportunities to reduce heat island effects by converting surface parking areas to natural planting areas or areas with lower heat retention properties.

[06:13:59] The ability to reduce parking requirements encourages the repurposing of surface parking areas, resulting in opportunities to better manage stormwater and mitigate pollution in the form of runoff, all of which supports the goals of CCAB and Resilient Fairfax. Reducing the influence of parking in high-density areas creates opportunities to build compact developments with quality pedestrian infrastructure that allows people to travel by non-vehicular modes to access goods, services, and employment, resulting in reduced overall vehicle emissions.

[06:14:29] Lastly, our project incentivizes land development practices that protect and restore natural resources through parking adjustment options that allow for reductions in required parking in exchange for conservation or enhancement of green space or tree canopies. The first three subsections of Article 6 cover applicability, parking standards, and the calculation of parking requirements.

[06:14:53] Since initiation of this project, it has been our goal to provide flexibility, reduce hurdles, and streamline administrative processes. These changes include administratively reducing parking to accommodate and incentivize for EVC installation or parking lot landscaping. We have also defined when a parking tabulation is required and created flexible options in calculating parking when a use changes.

[06:15:16] Currently, parking tabulations are required when a new use proposes to occupy an existing tenant space. Parking tabulations are time-consuming, expensive, and often burden or prevent small businesses from establishing a use. New provisions have been added requiring that accessible parking spaces be calculated using the base parking requirements.

[06:15:35] For example, an office located in a transit-oriented development would calculate its accessible parking space requirement using the base parking rate for offices, not the transit-oriented development rate showed in the tiered framework. Proposed minimum parking requirements or parking rates for all land uses in the zoning ordinance have been available to the public for almost a year, or actually for over a year.

[06:15:59] These rates were drafted following lengthy review by staff and our consultant, Nelson Nygaard, and the proposed changes are based on local and national data. A handful of rates include, notably, the single-family attached dwelling rate have been adjusted since initial release in response to community feedback.

[06:16:14] As has already been stated, our goal with regards to minimum parking rates was right-sizing and simplification. Many rates were standardized, and their calculation is based off a single variable, such as spaces per 1,000 square feet or number of spaces per employee. A byproduct of rate right-sizing and simplification is readability.

[06:16:34] The rates we are proposing are written in a way that makes them easier to understand and more predictable to measure. This slide shows two examples of parking rates that have been right-sized and simplified. The proposed tiered framework stratifies parking requirements based on the comprehensive plan and comprehensive plan designations.

[06:16:54] This framework addresses that a one-size-fits-all approach for parking is not appropriate for Fairfax County. Using this method, a large majority of the county will be subject to base parking requirements, while lower requirements are posed in areas with denser development or better transit availability.

[06:17:10] The tiered framework does not alleviate a property owner or developer from adhering to other zoning ordinance requirements such as maximum floor area ratio, minimum open space provisions, or landscaping requirements. This framework does support the goals of the comprehensive plan by reducing the influence of parking in urban or urbanizing areas of the county.

[06:17:32] Residential parking requirements have been a primary topic throughout this project. In summary, the current draft text proposes no changes to the base rate for single-family detached dwellings. Further, the proposed minimum requirements will only affect future development. For single-family attached and stacked townhomes, the current rate is retained with the addition of a newly required visitor parking requirement in common areas.

[06:17:57] Under the current ordinance, all required parking for a single-family attached or stacked townhome could be located within a dwelling's garage or in its driveway. The table shown here reflects the Planning Commission's recommendation for a multifamily dwelling unit parking requirement, which is higher than staff's recommended rate across all tiers, with a base requirement of 1.45 spaces per unit.

[06:18:20] The proposed multifamily minimum requirement for transit station areas, transit-oriented developments, and the PTC district remains bedroom-based. Based on our research, majority of multifamily dwellings within the TSAs, TODs, and the PTC districts are of one-bedroom types.

[06:18:36] Proximity to public transit as well as smaller unit types result in less car ownership in these areas. Currently, parking adjustments are in two subsections of Article 6 and divided between those which can be approved by the Director of Land Development Services and those that must be approved by the Board.

[06:18:54] We're proposing that all adjustments be consolidated into one subsection and proposing an expansion of potential adjustments. With community and staff input, this section was significantly revised as we progressed through the project. Our original proposal allowed administrative approval of all adjustment options.

[06:19:13] Currently, administrative approval is limited to shared parking and some transit-related adjustments up to 30%. All other reduction options are reserved for board approval. All proposed adjustments, whether considered by the Board or the Director, require an official request to be prepared and submitted by a professional engineer and are subject to technical review by staff with expertise in parking dynamics.

[06:19:37] Adjustments have defined criteria that must be demonstrated to qualify for consideration. No public hearing is required. However, adjustments associated with land use actions are addressed as part of the public hearings on the land use proposal. For shared parking, the current provisions allow shared and off-site parking opportunities consolidated into a new subsection.

[06:20:01] Revisions include a new option to calculate a standard shared parking adjustment for all uses identified in the zoning ordinance. The process to determine shared parking is based on industry criteria and is like that used by other local and Virginia jurisdictions. Where sites are served by transit, adjustments can be considered.

[06:20:19] New language provides a standard allowable transit adjustment of 10% for sites outside the tiered framework based on specific criteria. It also provides an option for a greater than 10% adjustment for uses located in or outside the tiered framework if minimum criteria expected in the standard reduction is met, and the applicant can meet at least two additional criteria.

[06:20:43] A new parking adjustment option for entirely affordable residential buildings is provided based on affordability criteria of 70% of area median income. National surveys from Parking Generation and other studies focused on affordable projects show that this type of development has a lower demand for parking.

[06:21:01] The adjustment permits the Board to take into consideration the context of the development and requires justification of the proposal. For public parking, currently parking adjustments are allowed based on availability of nearby public parking lots. This language is simplified to allow adjustments for any publicly maintained parking, including the potential for on-street metered parking.

[06:21:22] Further, it allows the availability of commercially operated public parking to be a justification for an adjustment request. New language is proposed to permit the Board to adjust the required parking for sites that possess elements of public benefit, such as historic designations or environmental elements worthy of preservation or enhancement, that may conflict with meeting the minimum parking requirements.

[06:21:48] This adjustment allows these factors to be considered in relationship to the parking requirements for a site. A modification is proposed to the current language for unique characteristics, allowing the Board to approve parking adjustments based on the consideration of the unique characteristics of both the use or the site.

[06:22:06] A new provision is added regarding potential adverse impacts of an approved parking adjustment. A parking utilization study may be required if the board or director determines that parking adjustment results in inadequate site parking or creates adverse off-site impacts, including an expectation of remedies if an adjustment does have this impact.

[06:22:27] These remedies are expected to rely on parking management actions rather than building additional parking. These actions can include valet parking, internal parking controls, or an off-site shared parking agreement to address peak parking demand. The loading standards are proposed for modification to simplify and modernize these requirements, which have not significantly changed since 1978.

[06:22:51] Currently, only 29 uses are identified in the zoning ordinance for specific loading space requirements. The loading requirements are proposed for grouping by use categories, and uses will be required to provide a loading space or an adequate receiving facility. Overall, the loading space requirements are proposed to be lowered, which reflects current practice and an analysis of frequently waived requirements.

[06:23:13] Lowering the loading space requirements will provide for flexibility in site design and potentially reduce impervious areas. The proposed amendment does not require a 15-foot wide by 25-foot long loading space for uses under 10,000 square feet. When loading spaces are not required, an adequate receiving facility will be required and can include a reserved parking space, pull-off areas, or other concepts to accommodate short-term loading without impacting parking or traffic circulation.

[06:23:41] These adequate receiving facilities are intended to serve as short-term loading areas for smaller delivery vehicles, food delivery, and ride-hailing vehicles that will not use a traditional loading facility. Staff has continued to refine the adequate receiving requirements throughout the project.

[06:23:59] Due to their size, loading spaces are typically in the rear of buildings and don't facilitate quick pickup and drop-off, which occurs more frequently than loading dock activities. An adequate receiving facility will locate these activities near the front of the business or residential building, reducing the blocking of parking spaces, travel aisles, or accessible spaces.

[06:24:20] The project team worked with the Fairfax County Department of Transportation and our consultant to develop the proposed minimum bicycle parking requirements. This is the first time bike parking is being required as part of the zoning ordinance. Previously, it was negotiated on a case-by-case basis at the rezoning entitlement stage.

[06:24:38] Nationally, minimum bicycle requirements are a relatively new element of municipal regulations, which limits our experience on effective requirements. If adopted, we expect to revisit the regulations after assessing their initial effectiveness. The required number of bicycle parking spaces is based either on a percentage of the required baseline auto parking spaces or a minimum number of spaces.

[06:25:00] In both cases, the minimum bicycle parking requirement increases as automobile parking minimums decrease within a tiered framework, reflecting enhanced abilities to use this mode of transportation within higher density and intensity development areas. Overall, minimum bicycle requirements are expected to encourage more biking as riders are more assured that bicycle parking is available for their use at a destination.

[06:25:26] During the outreach, we heard a lot of concern related to parking lot landscaping and heat out of effect. As the Board knows, a companion amendment for transitional screening and parking lot landscaping is on target to be covered with the Board early next year. This screen depicts the potential effects of the combination of these two amendments.

[06:25:44] Left is existing requirements for a 100,000 square foot office building, and the right is the potential change of parking re-managing and the proposed landscaping amendments. The right size of the parking lots, there is potential for more open space. Even with reduced parking lot size, we expect to see more trees because of the amendments for landscaping.

[06:26:01] You also see the introduction of pedestrian routes being required. Now, obviously, there's many ways to design a building and parking lots. This is just one example, and this example is not maximizing the utility of the site. In this scenario, the right size of a parking lot, there's potential for the lot to maximize the site potential and building size of additional 20,000 square feet and the required additional parking.

[06:26:25] However, maximizing the potential of the lot has never been the driver behind parking rates. Our focus has been right sizing rates and help encourage additional open space. While additional open space is not a requirement for the zoning ordinance, but we know that over-required parking will never provide for increased open space.

[06:26:44] Right-sizing parking in the base rates and the tiered framework is an important evolutionary step for the county. Flexibility is necessary to allow proposed uses to meet a realistic parking need for their operation. This may require less parking, but it could also result in more parking than expected.

[06:27:01] Experience with other municipalities who have lowered or eliminated minimum parking requirements shows that, when given the choice, most developers choose to provide parking. This parking has, in many cases, been less than originally required, but frequently more than the minimum requirement.

[06:27:17] Our experience in Tyson's is similar. Since 2011, some uses have had minimum parking requirements eliminated, others reduced significantly, yet developers have chosen to provide parking, even when no parking is required, and have constructed parking above minimum requirements.

[06:27:34] Increasing parking requirements creates incentives for better project design and innovative approaches for land development. In today's county experience, it is literally the difference between the Mosaic District and Fair Oaks Mall. It is rethinking development in terms of space for people rather than space for cars.

[06:27:54] Nationally and regionally, the relationship between parking and community values has gained prominence, and parking reform has been adopted or taken into consideration in large and small communities, as well as communities like ours that offer a diverse and robust development pattern and economy.

[06:28:11] Reforming parking will only enhance these elements. Our proposals are modest and targeted. The greatest innovation is targeted to areas that will realize the greatest benefit from reduced minimum requirements. However, overall, both right-sizing and administrative modifications will reduce the burden of meeting a minimum parking requirement and other arcane and inflexible regulations on uses.

[06:28:34] Our proposals will also help us meet many of the values and goals of One Fairfax, which are key to our county's sustainability. On September 13, 2023, the Planning Commission unanimously approved a recommendation to adopt the proposed changes to the ordinance with amendments and follow-on motions.

[06:28:57] I'd like to briefly discuss two of the Planning Commission's amendments. An amendment was approved that will raise the minimum multifamily rate from the staff recommendation of 1.3 spaces per unit to 1.45 spaces per unit. Information gathered from ITE's parking generation data, which is collected nationally and contains survey data from the 1980s through the 2010s, supports this rate of 1.3.

[06:29:21] As part of our consideration, we specifically sought to be conservative for this baseline rate, and ITE's parking demand data is conservative. In fact, we do have local survey data from suburban multifamily development that shows a lower parking demand rate, including the existing residences at Government Center Project.

[06:29:39] The proposed 1.3 spaces per unit represents the properly right-sized parking rate for suburban multifamily development. The Planning Commission approved an amendment that requires a parking adjustment for an approved land use entitlement to be considered by the Board.

[06:29:55] This requirement does not exist today. Since 2017, staff has administratively processed eight post-zoning parking adjustments, keeping ongoing approved development proposals moving forward in an expeditious manner while meeting the goals of right-sizing parking. Often, opportunities for reasonable adjustments aren't realized until site plan submission or when uses are being established.

[06:30:18] A goal of this project is to make the parking process smoother and shorter. Reasonable administrative reductions are a means to achieve this. We enthusiastically support this project, and we believe our changes will have a positive effect for the county. We thank you for your attention and constructive assistance and feedback as we've progressed with reimagining parking over the past two years.

[09:12:47] Data collection should be designed to help evaluate whether further modifications to the ordinance are warranted.
